Q1 asked whether a change to the wording of a comment, or to whitespace, in a file bound as a whole changes its code. On 2026-11-24 the owner chose none of the options and ruled that the approach must depend on the real effect of an edit (the owner: «Нужно подходить дифференцированно, в зависимости от реального эффекта правок. Правка форматирования должна считаться изменением, достойным внимания рецензента и ревьювера, только там, где это влияет на логику. Например, отступы в yaml — да, отступы в ts — нет. Правки в комментариях нужно проверять на соответствие (но не блокировать, а предупреждать) в случае проблем с ними. Опять же с исключением для SpecTag — он должен обеспечивать правильную связь»). The owner has not yet agreed to any node of the group. The ruling adds a judgement to those that the independent reviewer makes, so `docs/principles.md` was changed first. On 2026-11-24 the owner agreed («согласен») to the new wording of its paragraph on the reviewer, option A: the reviewer also judges, for every commit that adds or changes a comment in the code, whether each such comment is consistent with the code that it describes and with the rules that this code implements, and a finding about such a comment is only a warning. The cost that the owner accepted is that a commit that changes only comments also starts a paid run of the reviewer. `docs/principles.md` now holds that wording, and the decision goes into the message of the commit that ends the session. The rest of the ruling needs no change to any principle. A change to whitespace counts as a change to code only where the language of the file makes whitespace significant, as indentation is in YAML and in Python. The SpecSheriff tools are to contain, for common formats, descriptions of how comments are written and of where whitespace is significant. In a format for which they have no description, every edit still counts as a change to code. A change to a SpecTag still counts as a change to the code that the SpecTag binds. The drafting agent carried out the ruling and applied the new principle with fix-group3-e.mjs. The reviews leave one question for the owner. Rule 1.5.2 counts any change to a file bound as a whole as a change to its code, while rule 9.4 says that rewording a comment changes no code, and the SpecSheriff tools cannot recognise a comment in a file that is bound by SpecTags at its start. Two other points were settled by earlier rulings. Under the ruling of 2026-11-18 on row 10.1.27c, `sheriff guard` also refuses to run where the installed plugin lacks an extension that the project has chosen. - the agent sends no message between two such pages.

Stage 1 was the first stage of the step that changes the SpecSheriff tools. On 2026-11-20, after seeing the plan at https://claude.ai/artifact/9DDLzVwkHXMDzvQHaYpqcQ (move-plan.html), the owner told the agent to start that step («начинай»). Stage 1 built the new specification files in a staging folder, .ai_docs/t25-pass/moved/. The files under specs/ stay untouched until the SpecSheriff tools accept the new form of the guidelines (stage 3). The sub-steps of stage 1 are listed in order, each marked as done:

1. [x] The schema in moved/specs/_schema/ describes the following: a node of the kind `TableRule` with its columns, its groups and its rows, each cell holding text or a node without a title (node 8.6, s-5fewsp22mmdz7y6, and node 1.3.5, s-5ah25f6gy99panq); a node's `examples`; the `schema` property of a RecordType; a file for an extension of the guidelines.
2. [x] build-moved.mjs writes moved/specs/_guidelines/spec.json (sections 1 to 10) and one file for each extension in moved/specs/_guidelines/extensions/ (Domains, section 11; RecordType, section 12; Views of the specification, section 13). The structure and the order come from `parts` in base-structure.html; each rule's title, text, reason, examples and list are copied word for word from newform.json; the three tables come from table-10-1.json, table-11-3.json and table-12-12.json, and each of their rows that remains in force becomes a node in a cell, with a new sid; every node that has a draft keeps the sid in its draft's `sidValue`; every node is written with the approval status `draft`, and agreed.json lists each node that the owner has agreed to, with the date of the agreement, for the approvals to be recorded in stage 3.
3. [x] Links: each `cites` link from the citations in a node's text, reason, examples and list; the other typed links of a node that keeps its sid, from specs/_guidelines/spec.json; each row's `checks` links from its `rests`; the typed links that the drafts' lists of changes call for when the drafts are moved, such as a `refines` link from 1.7.7 to 10.1.
4. [x] The archive: the current rules that the new form drops are moved to specs/_archive/ with the date, the reason and the sid of the node that replaces that rule, because these rules were approved. Eleven such rules were found on 2026-11-20: 1.10, 1.15, 1.18, 1.28, 1.29, 1.32, 1.37, 1.39, 5.3, 6.5 and 6.6 in the current numbering.
5. [x] `meta.decisions` of each node: the decisions its draft already holds, the decisions of the current node with the same sid, and the owner's rulings recorded in the draft's list of changes, all of them written in English. EngStickler's reader agents read them.
6. [x] The text of each section: its purpose, from `parts`, written in English in section-purposes.json; EngStickler's readers read it once the build is done.
7. [x] The check of the build's output checks that: each sid occurs once; each citation and each link leads to an existing node; each agreed text is copied word for word from its draft or its row; the links from tools.json to the dropped rules are listed so that stage 2 can handle them.

Progress on 2026-11-20: a build agent wrote build-moved.mjs and the files in moved/, with moved/report.md. Four reader agents wrote the owner's rulings in English into decisions-out-1.json through decisions-out-4.json. Running the build revealed contradictions between agreed drafts, and two of them needed a ruling from the owner:
- The reason for rule 1.6.1 cited the current rule 5.3, which the new form drops. The owner accepted a sentence that states the reason directly.
- Rule 7.2.7 cited the node that defines the name RecordType in the RecordType extension, a citation that rule 1.7.3 forbids. The owner accepted SpecTag as the example name in place of RecordType.

The second pass of the build removed the links from the base to the extensions and merged 327 decisions into `meta.decisions`. It also wrote the section texts and the archive of the eleven dropped rules, and translated the todos written in Russian into English. In rules 1.3.2 and 1.3.3, the citation of rule 5.3 was replaced by a citation of rule 1.6.1, a technical replacement. The drafts 1.3.1 and 6.1 were given the `TextRule.list` of the current nodes 1.5 and 2.2, because the format of the drafts had no field for a list; each list only restates its agreed text. The third pass, started at 2026-11-20T18:32:12Z, makes the schema match the agreed drafts:
- an archived node has no sid;
- each extension has a schema of its own;
- the `ratio` of a link between terms is written in the notation that rule 6.6 of the new form (s-bckzdwd35d2nefa) defines;
- the link types are those in the list of rule 6.1, which does not hold `consumes`; `consumes` is added by the schema of the Domains extension;
- the `level` of a domain's header is optional.

Stage 1 was completed at 2026-11-20T18:50:28Z. Thirteen EngStickler readers read the 18,000 words of English that stage 1 added: the decisions, the section texts, the translated todos and the `of` field of the two restored lists. fix-stage1-english.mjs applied the 130 corrections that the readers proposed and the agent confirmed, and build-moved.mjs was run again. The files in moved/ still pass Ajv, and every agreed wording is still copied word for word from its draft or its row.

**Questions for the owner that arose from the inventory**, asked one at a time, each with a proposal: (1) rule 6.4 (s-bpdqd9v72wg7vsz) and rule 2.1 (s-a4rbry4hsu34hy8) together prevent the links from the appendix of the tools from being mirrored in the guidelines, which the reason for rule 2.2 relies on; propose-6-4.mjs proposed that a node of the guidelines and a node of an extension that the project has chosen also hold the links from the appendix, and the owner accepted it on 2026-11-20 («ок»); (2) the word "documents" is used both for the markdown files outside specs/ and for the files of a domain (X5 in stage2-inventory.md); the owner accepted "documentation" for the first sense in row 10.1.39 on 2026-11-20 («ок»); (3) row 10.1.17 exempts abbreviations from the ban on sentences written in capitals, while its deterministic criterion counts every word written in capital letters (P5); (4) row 10.1.26 says that each SpecTag names a rule, while a generated type carries the SpecTag of its RecordType, which is not a rule (P6). The agent settled question (3) without asking the owner: rule 7.6.2 (s-m6upmfq64kw5h4r) already forbids three or more words in a row written in capital letters, abbreviations included, so the criterion of row 10.1.17 checks exactly what 7.6.2 requires, and the row needs no change. Question (4) was put to the owner on 2026-11-20: should the RecordType extension state that a RecordType is a rule? The owner then asked the agent to explain how, in its view, a RecordType is tied to its tests and its code. The page recordtype-rule/index.html (https://claude.ai/artifact/EMKvRer7iZo1UKBwLkLeku) answers the owner's question about the chain and compares three ways of settling question (4): a RecordType is a rule; a separate rule states what the data is for, and the RecordType holds only the format of the data; a RecordType is not a rule. The owner chose the first way on 2026-11-21 («Согласен с вариантом А»). propose-12-3a.mjs drafted the new node 12.3a, "A RecordType is a rule". specsheriff-rule-reviewer checked the draft, and revise-12-3a.mjs applied its findings: the node says that a RecordType's fields state what it requires, that its description of what it is for serves as its reason, that the code generated from it is bound to it, and that at least one test checks that a data record that violates it is neither output nor stored. The statement about the reason refines rule 1.2.3. The owner agreed to node 12.3a on 2026-11-21 («ок»). After the step that changes the SpecSheriff tools, the tools will allow a test to carry the SpecTag of a RecordType and will require such a test for every approved RecordType. The tools currently reject such a test. The agent decides the other questions of the inventory on its own: each check of the appendix implements the rows of a table of checks that it carries out; the hooks and the commands implement nodes 10.2 and 1.6.1; one node of the appendix says how the tools load the extensions that a project has chosen; each replacement of a renamed term is a technical replacement.

**Stage 2 starts** with an inventory of the appendix of the tools, which an agent writes into stage2-inventory.md. It gives each file's path relative to the folder of the SpecSheriff tools in the plugin, and its path in the repository where the SpecSheriff guidelines are written.

- The fifth node is "A removed node and the archive" (s-j4webte36gkraju, tools 7.1.6). propose-archive.mjs drafted the node's new wording, and tools-drafts.before-archive.json keeps the drafts as they were before that script ran. The draft says the following:
  - `sheriff validate` reads each removed node, with its sid, from the commit before the removal, and recognizes the node's entry in the archive by the location and the kind that the removed node had;
  - a node goes to the archive only if it has ever been approved and its approval status is set by approvals, which excludes a section and a domain's header;
  - each node that a commit adds to the archive must be the archive entry of such a removed node, since a node of the archive has no sid by which its approvals could be found later;
  - a file of the archive is validated against `archive-file.schema.json` alone, and that schema describes only what the tools read from a node of the archive or require of it, as the owner ruled on 2026-11-12;
  - when a replacement is removed later, `sheriff reindex` names the replacement's own replacement in its place, as rule 2.6 requires.
- The agent asked the reviewer to judge four choices in that draft: recognizing an entry by its location and kind rather than by its wording; passing a replacement on in `sheriff reindex`, since no row of the main table of checks verifies that a replacement is a node of the specification; removing, in `sheriff reindex`, the sid of a node that an earlier release archived with a sid; and refusing a node that was never approved only in the commit that adds it to the archive.
- specsheriff-rule-reviewer found that draft not ready. Its findings:
  - The node is to be split into three, as rule 7.3.3 calls for: the check made when a commit removes a node, the validation of a file of the archive against its schema, and updating the reference to a replacement that was itself removed. The agent splits a node without asking, as .claude/vt/rules.md allows.
  - Having only `sheriff reindex` pass a replacement on does not hold every participant to rule 2.6, since the git hooks do not run `sheriff reindex`. A commit made without an agent would leave in the archive a replacement that the specification no longer contains. The reviewer recommends that row 10.1.11 also require that a node of the archive name only a node of the specification as its replacement, checked by `sheriff validate`. That changes an agreed row, so the agent has put it to the owner.
  - "Adds to the archive" is to be defined for nodes without a sid, so that an entry that `sheriff reindex` has rewritten does not count as added.
  - An entry is to hold the removed node's wording too, since row 10.1.11 requires the node itself to be placed in the archive.
  - The opening sentence is to say what is checked: that a commit adds to the archive exactly the nodes it removes that must be archived. The check must also leave out a node moved to another file.
  - A domain's header is to be removed from the node, as the note of 2026-11-12 says, since rule 1.3.8 speaks only of a section. The Domains extension has no rule saying that a header's approval status is derived from the nodes under it; that gap is to be brought up with the owner when the extension is next changed.
  - "The commands" (s-f5rz6b7dhxejjts) is to say that `sheriff reindex` removes the sid of a node that an earlier release archived with one, before it validates the archive. archive-file.schema.json and its comments are to change with this node.

- specsheriff-rule-reviewer's second review asked for the following fixes, which fix-archive-c.mjs made:
  - in "A removed node and the archive", a node of the archive counts as added by a commit after which the archive holds more nodes with that node's location, kind and wording than the archive of the commit it follows holds, each archive being read in the form in which `sheriff reindex` would write it. So a node removed again after it was restored counts, a node whose kind `sheriff reindex` renames does not, and a commit that changes an archived node's wording is refused. For a merge, the commit it follows is the merge's first parent, and for a node in a cell only the wording in the node's own fields is compared. The reviewer proposed an example, which is shown to the owner with the node;
  - in "Validation of a file of the archive", `sheriff binding` is dropped, since it reads nothing from the archive in the new form. The schema of the archive requires only that a node of the archive have a location, a kind and the `archived` field, and hold no sid, no node under it and no link pointing at it. It admits every other property in any form, since `sheriff reindex` changes what an earlier release wrote in the archive too;
  - no row of the main table of checks includes a check that a node of the archive holds no sid. The agent treats the validation of a file of the archive as part of row 10.1.11's check, and says so when the node is shown to the owner.

By the owner's ruling of 2026-11-21 on that node, if the SpecSheriff tools cannot run, the hook runs all the project's own tools in the bytewise order of their names.

If they do, "What the SpecSheriff tools read in the project's own guidelines" gains an item for it.
- In stage 3, `fixtureGlobs` in model.ts is to read only `project.json`, as `levelsOf` does, and `levelsOf` is to read the lists of all the rules on levels together rather than only the last.
- "No copy of the SpecSheriff guidelines" (s-6bmnsbskg6en4nf) is to name the files of the extensions of the guidelines, and the schemas of those extensions, among the copies it refuses.
- "The schema and a project's extension" (s-xzcw3hv4hmxz7h7) is to say that `extension.schema.json` checks the schema of each extension of the guidelines, as well as the project's extension.
- The nodes of the two plugins (s-zh2vcr3xjumppg5, s-2pdy9gw2jrjvfb4) are to list the extensions of the guidelines, their schemas and the appendix of the tools among what each plugin carries.
- The nodes s-f5rz6b7dhxejjts, s-j9r8z4xdrfkfs47, s-v4qus7raqnwzneb and s-7unxytshhc4md54 still say that the specification's approval status is read from a rule in `project.json`, and binding.ts, push-mode.ts, reindex.ts and generated.ts still read that rule; they are to say that it is read from the entries in `sheriff.config.json`.
- "How an approval is recorded" (s-v4qus7raqnwzneb) and "Building the map" (s-rpv4fkdqqguptqd) are to cite "Where the files are" for the paths of the approvals and of the map, which that node now holds.
- A defect: the pre-push hook refuses a push when `sheriff.config.json` names no `testCommand` and the specification's approval status is `approved`, regardless of what the project's own guidelines say about where the tests run. **Now (2026-11-20, 18:12 UTC):** the step of T25 that changes the SpecSheriff tools has begun. The agent decided the order of the stages of this step on its own, since the order of the work is the agent's to decide (the `vt` rule "Technical and procedural questions are the agent's to decide"). The agent derived that order from the principle "The specification first, in one direction":
1. The drafts in newform.json and in the tables of checks are moved into the specification files, with their `meta.decisions`. The schema in `specs/_schema/` is part of the specification, as node 5.1 states, so `TableRule` and the `schema` property of a RecordType are added to the schema in the same stage.
2. The nodes of the appendix of the tools are reviewed with the owner and changed, one node at a time.
3. The tests are written, then the code.
4. The skills and the guides are brought into line with the new specification, a release is rehearsed and published, and the saloon is updated to the new release.

Neither the current schema nor the current SpecSheriff tools accept a node of kind `TableRule`. Between the move and the change to the tools, the checks will therefore report failures, and the pre-commit hook may refuse the commit that closes a session. That commit is never made with `--no-verify`. Where the hook refuses it, the agent tells the owner, and the drafts stay in `.ai_docs/`, outside the specification files, until the tools accept the new form of the specification.

The move starts by reconciling the two versions of 10.1 (s-jau82aq9juv9zzh). The head of the table in table-10-1.json is kept, since the owner agreed to it in the form of a `TableRule`. The draft in newform.json is the old node of kind `TextRule`, whose text held the two tables; the owner changed its reason on 2026-11-17. The head's list of changes, accepted on 2026-11-11, says that the explanation of why some checks report a failure instead of refusing the commit is to be moved into those checks' reasons. reconcile-10-1.mjs records the reconciliation in the head's list of changes, and table-10-1.before-reconcile.json keeps the table as it was before the reconciliation. The paragraphs of the old reason are handled as follows:
- the paragraph on drift, where a rule's tests or code lag behind its text, was to become row 10.1.40's reason; the owner's answer reopened the row itself, as the paragraph after this list describes;
- "An approved rule that is not implemented is reported rather than refused, since a rule is approved before its test and its code are written (§s-rah8b37kytb682y)" is to become row 10.1.41's reason, to be proposed after row 10.1.40 is settled; the row's node then needs a `cites` link to s-rah8b37kytb682y;
- "A check reads the commit being made, because judging anything else would pass or refuse what is not being committed" becomes the reason of the node "The hooks" (tools/6) of the appendix of the tools when the owner reviews that node;
- the paragraph on code and tests without a SpecTag is not moved, since node 9.5 (s-kkbqcb9xrpaepwn), which rows 10.1.26 and 10.1.26a cite, already gives the reasons for it.

Row 10.1.40 is under discussion. On 2026-11-19 the owner read its approved wording, "The tests and the code of an approved rule do not lag behind its text, nor its code behind its tests, as the history of the repository shows", together with its cost on failure, "reported", as a contradiction. The owner said what had been meant: tests and code strictly follow the rule, and a lag is a reason to refuse the commit, except while the rule itself or the whole specification is a draft. The owner also asked for the reason to be written in a technical style, without phrases such as "not the fault of whoever made the commit". reopen-10-1-40.mjs withdrew the proposed reason from the page of the main table of checks. In the English wording of the row, "nor its code behind its tests" needs to be changed to "nor does its code lag behind its tests"; this will be fixed once the row is settled.

The agent first recommended keeping "reported", with three arguments. The owner answered each of them on 2026-11-19, and the agent's answers are now with the owner:
- A change in a rule's wording that keeps its meaning. The owner said that this is a defect in the check, not in the intent, and that the check must judge by meaning, not by dates. Where a rule's wording changes without changing its meaning, the agent records in the commit message that the tests and the code need no change, and the check then finds no lag. The agent agrees. The method already provides for this judgement: a change that does not change a rule's meaning is approved on the owner's behalf and reported to the owner (docs/principles.md). The agent proposes recording it as a git trailer in the commit message that names the rule; the commit-msg hook already reads the message. Still open: who checks that such a record is true. The reviewer judges only commits that change code, and the principle on deterministic checks names the reviewer's judgement as the only judgement that is not deterministic. The same record is needed where a test is added for behaviour the code already has, since the tests then change and the code does not.
- Several tests in one file. The owner said that this is a defect in the script and that the check must track each test, not each file. The agent agrees, and adds that a code file also holds the symbols of several rules. The SpecSheriff tools already map the changed lines of a commit to the symbols they belong to, for the check of the commit message, so the history can show the last commit that changed a rule's own tests and symbols. This will be fixed first in the appendix of the tools, then in the tests and the code of this step.
- Work stopping. The owner did not understand the argument and asked for it to be explained. The agent's second explanation stated two consequences of refusing a commit that leaves a rule's tests or code lagging behind its text. First, the specification could not be approved while the tests or the code of any approved rule lag. Second, the commit that approves a new wording of a rule would have to contain that rule's new tests and code. The owner answered both on 2026-11-19. A check never reads what the commit does not contain: the old text of 10.1 says so, and the list of changes in the head of the table says that the sentence is to be moved to the node "The hooks" of the appendix of the tools. A commit that only changes the approval status of the specification therefore leaves unchecked every rule outside the commit, and the first consequence does not arise. Where the commit includes a rule because something in the rule changed, the rule's tests and code must be brought into line with its new text in the same commit, so the second consequence is what the owner intends. The agent agrees. Its argument rested on the fact that the current SpecSheriff tools check the history of every node at every commit (the node s-txbwsthdqhvyzd2 of the appendix of the tools). That behaviour contradicts the rule that a check never reads what the commit does not contain, and will be fixed in this step of T25.

Below is the owner's view of how work on a project is done, given on 2026-11-19, when the owner was asked who checks that a change keeps a rule's meaning. The agent is now examining it with the owner using the skill `grilling`: one question at a time, each with the agent's recommended answer.
- A new application: the specification is written as a draft, its base and its technical appendix, and then approved; the agent then plans and implements all of it, or one domain at a time while the other domains' rules are still being agreed. While implementing, the agent writes the details of the implementation into the technical appendix and asks the owner about the base where needed.
- Maintenance: a request for a change arrives; the agent changes the rules concerned. Where a change alters a rule's logic, the tests and the code follow. Where a change only rewords a rule and keeps its logic, the agent records that the tests and the code need no change.
- The owner proposes an RFC (request for change; not the IETF's "request for comments"): a temporary document that the agent writes once the change has been discussed with the owner. It states the meaning of the change and its blast radius: the rules, the tests and the code that must change, without saying how. The checks also read it, possibly in a fixed format, one that would list the checks to run.

How this view compares with the guidelines, as the agent told the owner:
- What an agent may implement is decided by each rule's approval, not by the specification's: a draft rule is not implemented (10.1.28a), whereas an approved rule may be implemented even while the specification is still a draft. The specification's approval decides whether the tests must pass on a push (10.1.44) and whether a warning is issued for code without a SpecTag (9.5).
- A node the agent adds to the technical appendix while implementing is a draft until an owner approves it, so its code cannot be committed until then (the principle "Nothing unapproved is implemented").
- The guidelines have no change document. A change that does not alter a rule's meaning is approved on the owner's behalf and reported (docs/principles.md), but nothing records that its tests and code need no change. A commit message names the approved nodes that the changed symbols implement (9.6). CLAUDE.md says that a decision is never kept in a document of its own: a decision on a rule goes into that rule's `meta.decisions`, and any other decision into the commit message.

The first question of the grilling session, now put to the owner, is whether a change needs a document of its own. The agent recommends not having a separate document. That the meaning is unchanged is recorded where the change is approved, as an approval made on the owner's behalf for a wording that keeps the meaning; once T28 is done, that approval is kept in the node. The blast radius is computed by the SpecSheriff tools from the links between nodes and from the SpecTags, and shown as a report, rather than written by hand.

The owner then asked (2026-11-19) where the mark "the meaning is unchanged" would be kept without a separate document, and how a deterministic check could detect the agent's decision. The agent's answer:
- The mark would be kept in a file in the commit, not in the session. Today `sheriff approve <sid>` appends an entry to `specs/_approvals.jsonl`, which is committed together with the changed rule. The entry names the sid, the fingerprint of the wording and who approved it.
- A new field would be added to the entry. An option of the same command sets it when the approver states that the new wording keeps the meaning of the previously approved wording. The names of the field and of the option have yet to be settled.
- When a commit is being made, for each approved rule whose wording the commit changes, the check looks at the rule's own tests and symbols in the commit. Where none of them has changed, it looks in the commit for the approval entry with that sid and fingerprint. With the field, the commit passes. Without it, the commit is refused, and the refusal message names the rule.
- Once T28 is done, the same field is kept in the approval stored in the node.
- The check verifies that the mark exists, not that it is correct. Who verifies that it is correct is a separate question in the grilling session.

The owner answered the first question on 2026-11-19: what mattered was that the decision is recorded in a place where a script can find it, and the approval entry is such a place. So a change needs no separate document: the mark "the meaning is unchanged" is recorded in a field of the approval entry, and the blast radius is computed by the SpecSheriff tools.

The second question of the grilling session was who verifies that the mark is correct. The agent recommended that the independent reviewer verify it. An agent that approves, on the owner's behalf, a change that only rewords a rule both decides that the meaning is unchanged and approves the change, so it certifies its own work. The reviewer would judge whether the rule's existing code and tests do what its new wording says, which is the judgement it already makes for changed code. That requires extending the principle on deterministic checks, which has the reviewer judge "at every commit that changes code", to cover a commit that changes the wording of a rule bound to code. The other options put to the owner were to trust the approval, since an approval made on the owner's behalf is reported to the owner, who can withdraw it, or to let only an owner set the mark personally.

On 2026-11-19 the owner chose to have the independent reviewer verify the mark stating that the rule's implementation needs no change (option 1). So the principle on deterministic checks in docs/principles.md, under which the reviewer judges "at every commit that changes code", is also to be extended to cover a commit that changes the wording of a rule bound to code. The principle's new wording will be proposed to the owner once the grilling session has settled how a change is checked, together with the other wordings that follow from the grilling session.

The third question of the grilling session was whether the check should still compare a rule's tests with its code by the dates of their changes, that is, the part of row 10.1.40 that reads "nor its code behind its tests". The agent recommended dropping that comparison. Where a test is added for behaviour that the code already has, the tests change and the code does not, and nothing lags behind. Where the code does lag behind a test, the test fails when the tests are run, at the moment that the project's own guidelines specify (10.1.44), and the reviewer judges the commit that adds the test. The other option put to the owner was to keep the comparison and let a mark "the code needs no change", judged by the reviewer, settle the case.

The owner chose on 2026-11-19 to keep the comparison in a more precise form: only the tests bound to the rule in the commit are compared with only the code bound to that rule, so that the check follows how each rule is implemented rather than which files changed. The agent takes this to be the second option: where a rule's tests change in a commit and its code does not, the commit contains a mark stating that the code needs no change, and the reviewer judges that mark.

The fourth question of the grilling session was first put in this form: where the mark "the code needs no change" should be kept when only a rule's tests change, since such a commit changes no wording and so has no approval entry. The agent recommended a git trailer in the commit message, naming the rule.

The owner then asked (2026-11-19) why two places were proposed, the approval entry and the commit message. The agent's clarification: the agent had proposed two places for two cases. Where a rule's wording changes, the change is always approved, so the mark could be a field of the approval entry. Where only a rule's tests change, nothing is approved, so the mark has to be kept elsewhere. One place is simpler: a trailer saying that the implementation of the named rule needs no change in this commit covers both cases, and the approval entry then stays as it is. The commit-msg hook reads the trailer, since, of the hooks that check a commit, only the commit-msg hook sees the commit message.

The fourth question, as it was then put to the owner, was whether the mark should be kept in one place, a trailer in the commit message for both cases, or in two places, a field of the approval entry where the wording changes and a trailer where only the tests change. The agent recommended one place.

The owner chose one place on 2026-11-19 (option 1). As a result, the mark stating that the implementation of a named rule needs no change is a trailer in the commit message, in both cases, and the approval entry gains no field. The commit-msg hook reads the trailer, and the reviewer judges it. The choice of names for the trailer and its values is a technical decision, which the agent makes when the appendix of the tools is changed.

The fifth question of the grilling session was what happens, while the specification is a draft, to a commit that changes an approved rule's wording without changing either its tests or its code and without the trailer. The owner's exception lets the commit pass; the question was whether it passes silently or with a report naming the rule. The agent recommended a report. The check reads only the commit, so a lag that remains while the specification is a draft is never seen again by the check at a later commit that does not include the rule. The report on the commit that changes the wording is therefore the only occasion on which the lag becomes visible. Before the specification is approved, the approval procedure shows its owner what the checks report (10.6). The other option put to the owner was to let the commit pass silently, as node 9.5 provides for code without a SpecTag while the specification is a draft.

The owner chose the report on 2026-11-19 (option 1). While the specification is a draft, such a commit passes, and the check reports each rule whose implementation lags behind its new wording.

The sixth question of the grilling session concerned the owner's view that the agent writes the details of the implementation into the technical appendix while implementing. Under the principle "Nothing unapproved is implemented", each node the agent adds to the technical appendix is a draft until an owner approves it, and its code cannot be committed until then. The question was whether that should hold for such nodes. The agent recommended keeping the principle and having the owner approve the agent's appendix nodes together, on one page, for one piece of work. Every node of the technical appendix is a rule that the code implements, and the reviewer judges the code against it, so a node nobody approved would let the agent set the rule that its own code is then judged by. The other options put to the owner were to let the agent approve such nodes on the owner's behalf and report them, which changes the principle, or to require no approval for the technical appendix, which changes the principle for a whole part of the specification.

The owner chose on 2026-11-19 to let the agent approve such nodes on the owner's behalf and report them to the owner (option 2). The principle "Nothing unapproved is implemented" in docs/principles.md is therefore to be changed, and its new wording will be proposed together with the other new wordings that follow from the grilling session.

The seventh question of the grilling session was who checks a node of the technical appendix that the agent approved on the owner's behalf. The agent recommended that the independent reviewer check it, just as the owner had chosen the reviewer to verify the mark stating that the implementation needs no change. The reviewer would judge whether the node only spells out the approved base rule it implements and contradicts nothing in the base, as node 4.2 requires of every node of the technical appendix. Such a node is committed together with the code that implements it, and the reviewer judges that commit anyway, so no extra run of the reviewer is needed. The other option put to the owner was that the report to the owner be the only check on such a node, and that the owner withdraw an approval where necessary.

The owner chose on 2026-11-19 to have the independent reviewer check such nodes (option 1).

The eighth question of the grilling session was which nodes of the technical appendix the agent may approve on the owner's behalf, and how the SpecSheriff tools recognise them. The agent runs `sheriff approve` under the owner's git identity, so an approval does not show who made it. The agent recommended a boundary that the tools can check without knowing who approved the node: a node of the technical appendix qualifies for approval by the agent if it has an `implements` link to an approved rule of the base. The reviewer then judges every such node whose approval a commit contains, whoever approved it, and the report to the owner lists them. The other option put to the owner was that the agent mark each approval it makes on the owner's behalf with a trailer in the commit message, and that only the nodes whose approvals are marked in this way be reviewed and reported.

On 2026-11-19 the owner chose the boundary that the node's own links draw (option 1). The rules of the base and the nodes that have no `implements` link to an approved rule of the base may be approved only by an owner.

The grilling session has settled how a change is checked. As the `vt` rules "A design is drawn before it is planned" and "An architectural decision is weighed against best practice in its field" require, the agent drew the design as a page, change-check-design.html (https://claude.ai/artifact/D1pWHkY9z1VJQ3n9x7aeaQ), and compared each decision with established practice in a matrix on the same page, before proposing any wording. The owner looked at the page and replied "OK" on 2026-11-19. The wordings that follow from the design are proposed one at a time, for row 10.1.40, the two principles in docs/principles.md, node 1.3.6 (s-t5uw8zfxzhfdg86) on approvals made on an owner's behalf, node 10.4 (s-nyh9yk22ba6g77q) on what the reviewer is given, and node s-txbwsthdqhvyzd2 of the appendix of the tools.

The owner agreed to row 10.1.40 on 2026-11-19 («ок»). The agreed wording is the one rewritten after the owner's remark that the first proposal used a commit as the subject of the verb "change": a commit itself changes nothing, and the wording, the tests and the code are what change in a commit. accept-10-1-40.mjs wrote the agreed row into table-10-1.json:
- text: Where the wording of an approved rule changes in a commit, the tests and the code already bound to that rule change in the same commit. Where the tests of an approved rule change in a commit, the code already bound to that rule changes in the same commit. Instead of these changes, the commit message may state that the rule's implementation needs no change.
- on failure: refused, naming the rule, while the specification is approved; reported, naming the rule, while it is a draft
- reason: Without this check, an approved rule can enter the commit history with tests and code written for its earlier wording, and nobody notices. While the specification is a draft, the work is focused on the specification itself, so a rule whose tests and code lag behind its wording is only reported.
Row 10.1.41, not row 10.1.40, covers a rule that has no tests and no code yet, since the phrase "already bound" leaves such a rule out. "Bound to the rule" has the meaning that row 10.1.41 gives the phrase: directly, or through a rule that implements the rule in question. That definition is stated in row 10.1.41. Moving it into node 1.5.2, so that both rows rely on a single definition, is proposed when node 1.5.2 comes up for review.
The same construction, in which a commit is the subject of a verb that denotes a change, is used in rows 10.1.27, 10.1.27a, 10.1.27b and 10.1.28a, in the drafts of 10.2, 10.4 and 10.5, and in both the text and the reason of 9.5. Each of these is a row or a node of its own, so the agent proposes each to the owner separately when its turn comes.

Row 10.1.40 has two todos for the step that changes the SpecSheriff tools. The first says that `sheriff binding` must stop reporting the drift of a draft rule. The second lists what must change in the check that tools node s-txbwsthdqhvyzd2 describes:
- it reads only the rules whose wording or tests change in a commit;
- it compares the tests bound to a rule with the code bound to that rule, test by test and symbol by symbol, rather than file by file;
- it reads the trailer in the commit message;
- it refuses a commit in which a rule's tests or code lag behind its wording, or its code behind its tests, while the specification is approved.

The reviewer also found a terminology clash: 11.1.2 calls the files of a domain "documents", while the appendix of the tools (s-zefk589ykh37b8u, s-m8nmfsxkcfu7cjp) and row 10.1.39 call the markdown files outside `specs/` "documents". The agent proposes renaming the term "documents" to "documentation" in the appendix, as part of the step that changes the SpecSheriff tools. The owner agreed to the whole of table 12.12 on 2026-11-19. finish-12-12.mjs then renumbered the rows of table 12.12 in the order in which they are in the table, a mechanical change: the old rows 12.12.5 to 12.12.11 became 12.12.4 to 12.12.12 with the new rows among them, and the old rows 12.12.14 to 12.12.16 became 12.12.13 to 12.12.15; the new row on fragments of a format is 12.12.16. The table before the renumbering is kept in table-12-12.before-renumber.json. EngStickler's thorough reader read the English text of the agreed rows, and fix-12-12-english.mjs corrected it, together with the same phrases in 12.6 and 12.10.

The owner ruled that everything about RecordType follows JSON Schema 2020-12. The owner also accepted in advance every purely mechanical change, so that only the changes that are matters of principle are put to the owner. Both are written down in .claude/vt/rules.md. The first is the rule "A decision about a data structure is weighed against JSON Schema first". The second is a sentence in the reason for the rule "A change that only carries out a decision already made is not shown". The draft of 12.6 already records the ruling in its `decisions`. The ruling goes into the `meta.decisions` of every other node it changes when the drafts are moved into the specification files.

The page recordtype-jsonschema.html in t25-pass/ (https://claude.ai/artifact/LLgwm43UP8WCAcpN7FjrZg) shows the proposal that the owner accepted:
- the fields of a RecordType variant are at the same level as the fields of its RecordType, and the field of type `variant` is removed;
- the type of a field is written with the keywords of JSON Schema, with the same meaning as now;
- what JSON Schema cannot express stays in a vocabulary of SpecSheriff's own;
- the generated schema uses pairs of `if` and `then` instead of `oneOf` with a member for the remaining values.

Ajv 2020 accepted or rejected 13 sample records as expected (the scratchpad script jsonschema-variants.mjs).

To put this ruling into effect, the following was done:
- json-schema-recordtype.mjs wrote the mechanical changes as agreed. The drafts as they were before it ran are kept in newform.before-json-schema.json and table-12-12.before-json-schema.json.
- In 12.7, 12.8, 12.9 and 12.11, "a field of type `one of`" became "a field with `enum`". Rows 12.12.10 and 12.12.11 changed in the same way.
- In rows 12.12.2 and 12.12.3, "`identifier of`" became "`identifierOf`".
- Row 12.12.4 was removed. The numbers of the other rows stay as they are until the whole table is agreed.
- specsheriff-rule-reviewer checked the proposal for 12.6, and json-schema-12-6-review.mjs applied its findings.
- The owner agreed to 12.6 on 2026-11-19. In 12.6, a data record contains the fields of its RecordType variant alongside the fields of its RecordType. The name of each field that a variant declares differs from the name of every field of its RecordType.
- Together with 12.6, the owner agreed to row 12.12.7, "A RecordType variant declares at least one field". The owner also agreed to the new row after row 12.12.7, which checks the names of a variant's fields. The new row has no number yet.
- The English text of 12.2 now says "in terms that belong to no programming language" in place of "in technology-neutral terms" (fix-12-2-english.mjs). The owner's ruling of 2026-10-26 uses the names that JSON Schema gives to the bounds of a field. So the Russian «нейтральными словами» stays, and 12.2 remains agreed.
- english-12-6.md contains the English text of 12.6, of row 12.12.7, of the new row after it and of the changed item of 12.2. EngStickler's thorough reader read it, and fix-12-6-english.mjs corrected the English text of 12.6 without changing its meaning.

To put this ruling into effect, the step that changes the SpecSheriff tools must include the following changes, which add 5–7 h to its estimate:
- In node.schema.json, a RecordType contains a `schema` property, a JSON Schema object, instead of the properties `fields` and `unknown`.
- The type `variant` and the value `"where it holds a value"` of `required` are removed.
- The schema of a variant contains no `unevaluatedProperties`.
- The vocabulary of SpecSheriff is declared: `identifiedBy`, `identifierOf`, `chosenBy`, `answersTo` and the keyword for the source of gathered values.
- The SpecSheriff tools write the gathered values of a field into its `enum`. Alongside the field, they record the node that each value comes from, as 12.9 requires.
- types.ts generates the JSON Schema with pairs of `if` and `then`, with `$anchor` set to the sid and with `unevaluatedProperties`. TypeScript and Python get flat discriminated unions.
- validate.ts checks that the names of a variant's fields differ from those of its RecordType. This replaces counting the fields of type `variant`. Node tools/7.1.16 (s-c2r9jndrjbtbsv6) states the new check.
- Node tools/7.1.16 also states that a variant without a `refines` link is refused. validate.ts already refuses such a variant.
- Node tools/7.1.16 states that a RecordType variant is declared in the same section as its RecordType, instead of in the part that declares its value (row 12.12.6).
- The rule of the appendix of the tools on the neutral types names the keywords of JSON Schema 2020-12 and the vocabulary of SpecSheriff.
- The tests of the SpecSheriff tools, and the RecordType nodes of the saloon, are rewritten.
- Node 7.2.8 (s-dbk2vyeg8z5jxna) of the appendix of the tools must change. It requires the tools to refuse a RecordType with two fields of type `variant`.
- Nodes s-suv42g8r3ra2h6b, s-nk8td3ez58gahc4 and s-4npdup7mjkva69y must change too. The last of them has a row for the type `variant`.
- The tests listed in `meta.tests` of node s-jau82aq9juv9zzh must change.
- The step settles two questions raised by specsheriff-rule-reviewer. The first is whether a data record with a field of another variant is refused when its RecordType allows unknown fields. The second is whether the names of fields are compared regardless of letter case.

The earlier page recordtype-architecture.html (https://claude.ai/artifact/MxjiUi1H6XYCm2ndKzmcht) shows the current structure in a diagram. It compares six design decisions with TypeScript, Kotlin, Java, OpenAPI, JSON Schema, Protobuf, serde, UML and DDD. It marked JSON Schema as only partly supporting the choice of a variant by several fields, which was a mistake.

After row 11.3.7 come the step of T25 that changes the SpecSheriff tools, the move of the drafts into the specification files, and the step that changes the skills.

Agreed in the sessions of 2026-11-18 and 2026-11-19: section 14 removed; table 11.3, "The table of checks of the Domains extension"; 1.7.7, "The table of checks of an extension"; the names "the main table of checks" and "the table of checks of an extension", now used in 10.1, 1.6.1, 10.2, 10.6, 10.8, 11.3 and 12.12 in place of the old name; the definition of a part of the system in 12.1; the changes to 11.1.3, 11.2.1, 11.2.2, 12.1 (letter case) and 12.6 (the field of type `variant`, which the proposal for 12.6 now replaces). After table 12.12 come: the row for 11.1.2 (one section per document), the step of T25 that changes the SpecSheriff tools, the move of the drafts into the specification files with their `meta.decisions` (reconciling the two versions of 10.1, the draft in newform.json and the head in table-10-1.json), and the step that changes the skills, spec-adopt included.

The earlier state of 2026-11-18, kept below, records how the extensions came to be chosen in the repository's configuration.

The owner's earlier rulings found on the subject:
- 2026-11-09, the design of the extensions (extensions-design.html): `sheriff guide` prints the rules of the Domains extension where the project has domains. It also prints an index of the extensions, so that a project without domains learns that it can introduce domains. The choice was thus inferred from what the specification contains. Domains was then the only extension.
- 2026-11-13, 2.3: a project's guidelines consist of the SpecSheriff guidelines, the extensions the project has chosen and its own guidelines. Each extension goes into a file of its own, so that the agent reads only the chosen ones. The agent made that decision because the owner told it to decide («сам реши»).
- 2026-11-16, 2.4: where a value may differ between projects, only a project's own guidelines specify that value.
- 2026-11-17, the strict flag (10.7): the owner put the flag on the same level as the specification's approval status. Only an owner turns it on or off, and each such act is recorded in an entry that names that owner. The agent had recommended a rule of the project's own guidelines, citing 2.4, instead.

The agent first recommended that a rule of the project's own guidelines name the extensions the project uses. The other options put to the owner were an owner's entry, as with the strict flag, and inference from the specification's content, as in the design of 2026-11-09.

The owner answered on 2026-11-18: the settings of the plugin record which extensions exist and which are active now («в настройках плагина должно фиксироваться какие расширения есть и какие в данный момент активны»). The owner also asked what the procedure for enabling an extension should be, and whether best practices exist.

The agent's answer, now presented to the owner:
- The agent prefers the owner's choice to its own recommendation. The schema of the specification depends on which extensions are active, so the SpecSheriff tools must know which extensions are active before they read the specification. A choice kept in a node could only be read after the specification had been validated, and the validation itself depends on that choice.
- "The settings of the plugin" are taken to mean `sheriff.config.json` at the root of the repository. The plugin's own folder is outside git, so each machine would have a choice of its own. A file in the repository is shared by every clone and read by both agents' plugins and by the git hooks.
- Only the active extensions are recorded in `sheriff.config.json`. The available extensions come with the installed release of the plugin. A list of them kept in the project would go out of date with each update. A command of the SpecSheriff tools would list the available extensions and mark the active ones.
- Best practices: PostgreSQL separates the available extensions, which come with the server, from those installed in a database by `CREATE EXTENSION`. `DROP EXTENSION` is refused while objects depend on the extension. Sphinx, ESLint and Babel keep the list of active extensions in the project's configuration file in the repository. An unknown name in that list is an error. A tool refuses a repository whose git `extensions.*` settings require an extension the tool does not know, or a schema whose `$vocabulary` requires one. VS Code and Claude Code share a team's choice through a file in the repository rather than each user's settings.
- The proposed procedure: only a command of the SpecSheriff tools enables or disables an extension. `sheriff guard` refuses an agent's edit of the list of active extensions. Only an owner of the specification enables or disables an extension. The checks that run on each commit verify this, as they do for the strict flag and for approvals. Disabling is refused while the specification holds nodes of the extension's kinds, or while rules of the project's own guidelines link to the extension's rules. A name in the list of active extensions that matches no extension in the installed release makes the checks refuse every commit, naming the release that is needed. An extension has no version of its own, since it comes with the plugin's release.

The owner then asked (2026-11-18) whether a command in Claude Code and in Codex can take arguments. The agent answered from the documentation of both agents. A command of the SpecSheriff tools, such as `sheriff approve <sid>`, is run in a shell and takes arguments in either agent. In Claude Code, a skill invoked as a slash command receives its arguments through `$ARGUMENTS`, `$0`, `$1` and named arguments. In Codex, a skill is invoked by typing `$` followed by its name, or by choosing it from the list that `/skills` shows. The text after the mention reaches the model, but the documentation describes no placeholders. Codex's custom prompts accept `$1` to `$9` and KEY=value arguments, but they are deprecated in favour of skills. The agent then asked the owner again about the procedure.

The owner then proposed (2026-11-18) a skill, named `specsheriff-settings` or something similar, that shows the project's current settings in the terminal: the specification's approval status, the strict flag, and the extensions, marking the active ones. The skill would let the owner activate other extensions immediately, with checkboxes like those of Claude Code's `/plugin` screen. The agent's answer, now presented to the owner:
- The command `sheriff settings` of the SpecSheriff tools prints these settings. The commands `sheriff extension enable <name>` and `sheriff extension disable <name>` change the list of active extensions. The commands enforce the procedure, so the skill cannot do anything that the procedure forbids.
- The skill is named `spec-settings`, following the owner's ruling of 2026-11-03 (T23, de4010a) that the plugin's skills are named `spec` or `spec-*`.
- In Claude Code, the skill asks through the question dialog, whose options can be shown as checkboxes. The dialog cannot show an option as pre-checked, so the skill asks two questions: which inactive extensions to enable, and which active ones to disable. In Codex, whose documentation on skills describes no such dialog, the skill asks in the conversation.
- The `/plugin` screen is built into Claude Code itself, and a skill cannot display a screen of its own. The rule in 1.7 names neither the skill nor the commands, which belong in the appendix of the tools.

The owner agreed on 2026-11-18 that the skill `spec-settings` will be made («да скилл делаем»). The owner then asked how settings can be changed interactively. The interface the owner has in mind is a list of checkboxes, navigated with the arrow keys and toggled with Space or Enter, in the terminal or on a web page that sends the choice back. The owner mentioned, as an example, the brainstorming skill, which shows local pages of design variants in the browser. The options the agent found, now presented to the owner:
- The agents' built-in question dialogs. In Claude Code, the question dialog offers checkboxes, but it shows at most four options per question, with none of them pre-checked. Codex has the tool `request_user_input`, which asks a question with options in its terminal interface. Its documentation does not say whether several options can be selected. In both agents, the model turns the answer into commands, and that step is not deterministic.
- A terminal program in the SpecSheriff tools, `sheriff settings`: a list of checkboxes, with the active extensions pre-checked and an extension that cannot be disabled shown greyed out, with the reason given. It is exactly the interface the owner described. It is deterministic and works the same in Claude Code, in Codex and without an agent. It needs an interactive terminal: the agent's shell runs commands with no keyboard attached, so the owner runs it in a terminal window of their own.
- A local web page, served on 127.0.0.1 by the SpecSheriff tools, like the brainstorming skill's companion page or the owner's viewer of the specification. The page applies the change itself, through the same commands, without waiting for the agent's next turn. It is consistent with task T29, which moves the viewer into the plugin.
- Artifacts are excluded: they are a feature of claude.ai, Codex has none, and a page on claude.ai cannot change a file in the local repository.
The agent recommends the terminal program, with the skill showing the settings in the conversation and naming the command that changes them.

The owner chose the terminal program on 2026-11-18 («Программа в терминале - похоже наиболее подходящее решение») and asked for a test version first. settings-prototype.mjs in t25-pass/ is that prototype. It shows the specification's approval status, the strict flag, who the user is, and the extensions as a list of checkboxes. The arrow keys move the cursor, Space or Enter toggles an extension, and Enter on Apply ends the program. It changes nothing: it prints the commands the finished program would run. Every value except the user's git email address and the owners read from specs/_guidelines/project.json is sample data. The agent tested it with keystrokes piped into it. The prototype's handling of raw keyboard input in an interactive terminal has not been tested, since the agent's shell has no terminal.

The owner also asked whether the program works over Remote Control, since the owner was working from a browser on a laptop with Claude Code running on the desktop. It does not: the documentation of Remote Control says that commands that run only in the terminal interface, such as `/plugin`, work only from the local terminal. Remote Control forwards permission prompts and the question dialog's questions to the browser. So the agent proposes that the skill `spec-settings` also offer the question dialog, which works remotely, for times when the owner is away from the machine. The terminal program stays the main interface. Over an SSH connection to the desktop, the terminal program also works.

The owner then ran the prototype with the `!` prefix in Claude Code (2026-11-18). The program drew one screen and exited immediately with "Cancelled: nothing changed", and its colour escape sequences were printed as plain text. A command run with the `!` prefix gets no keyboard input and does not print to a terminal, which confirms that the list of checkboxes needs an interactive terminal. That cancellation without an explanation was a defect in the prototype. The agent fixed it: without an interactive terminal, the prototype prints the settings as plain text and says where the list of checkboxes can be used. The environment variable SHERIFF_PROTOTYPE_KEYS=1 still lets a test pipe keystrokes in. The agent's question of whether the skill should also offer the question dialog in the chat is still open.

The owner then asked (2026-11-18) whether the command can run the program in a separate terminal window. The agent tested this from its own shell: PowerShell's `Start-Process -Wait` opened a separate console window, a process in it had an interactive terminal for both input and output, and the call returned once the window had closed. So the skill can open `sheriff settings` in a separate window and report what was changed when the window closes. It has these limitations:
- the window opens on the machine where the agent runs, so over Remote Control it appears on the desktop's screen, which the owner working from a laptop cannot see;
- it needs a graphical desktop, so it does not work over an SSH connection alone or on a server without one;
- each operating system has its own command for opening a window, and on macOS it is harder to wait for the window to close;
- whether Codex's sandbox lets a command open a window has not been checked.
The agent proposes that the skill open the separate window when the session runs on a machine with a graphical desktop, and offer the question dialog in the chat when the owner works remotely. That proposal also covers the open question about the question dialog in the chat.

The owner also asked how this would work once the plugin is installed on a user's machine. The agent's answer: the installed plugin is a folder holding the skills and the built SpecSheriff tools. The skills already run the tools as `node "<plugin>/tools/dist/cli.js" <command>`. In Claude Code, the variable CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT holds the path to the folder, and in Codex the skill finds the folder from its own location (copy-assets.mjs rewrites that command line for Codex). `spec-settings` would work the same way: the agent prints the settings in the chat, then opens the same program in a separate window, or uses the question dialog when the owner works remotely. The change is written to `sheriff.config.json` in the repository and committed like any other change. The plugin needs nothing beyond Node, which the tools already require. A user who runs the program without an agent needs the full path to the plugin's folder, since `sheriff` is not on the PATH in a user's project. For this reason the skill prints the exact command. The plugin's options in Claude Code's `/config`, such as the reviewer's model, are per-user settings. The active extensions are a project-level setting, so they are kept in the repository instead.

The owner agreed on 2026-11-18 («да делаем») that the skill should open the program in a separate window on the user's machine and offer the question dialog in the chat when the owner works remotely. The owner added that the agent itself must be able to change the settings when a user asks it in the chat to do so, in any project that uses the plugin.

draft-1-7-choice.mjs then wrote three new drafts after 1.7.4, and put their rows on the page that shows the base:
- 1.7.5 "A project's active extensions" (s-9ttvcwpqkehm58g): the repository's configuration lists the active extensions, only they hold in the project, and the list names only extensions that the installed release delivers;
- 1.7.6 "Who enables or disables an extension" (s-e29p4xeq72drq5u): only an owner enables or disables an extension, each change names that owner, and an agent does so on an owner's behalf when that owner asks;
- 1.7.7 "An extension the specification depends on stays active" (s-j8y62pa9hzz6nwx): an extension stays active while a node of a kind that it adds exists, or while a node links to one of its rules. A request to disable it is refused, and the refusal names those nodes.
The wording checker and the agent specsheriff-rule-reviewer are checking the three drafts. The configuration file, its key, the commands, the separate window and the skill `spec-settings` are to be described in the appendix of the tools and in the skill, in the step of T25 that changes the SpecSheriff tools. Follow-up changes to agreed nodes will be proposed once the drafts are agreed: 5.1 (the schema also includes what the active extensions add), 10.3 (`sheriff guard` also covers the list of active extensions), and new rows of table 10.1.

specsheriff-rule-reviewer reported that the three drafts are not ready, and fix-newform-469.mjs applied the findings that concern wordings not yet agreed:

The refusal of a name that the installed release does not deliver goes into a new row of table 10.1, as is done for the refusals that enforce other requirements.
